Interpreting Lambda Calculus in Domain-Valued Random Variables
Robert Furber, Radu Mardare, Prakash Panangaden, Dana Scott

Abstract
We develop Boolean-valued domain theory and show how the lambda-calculus can be interpreted in using domain-valued random variables. We focus on the reflexive domain construction rather than the language and its semantics. The notion of equality has to be interpreted in the Boolean algebra and when we say that an equation is valid in the model we mean that its interpretation is the top element of the Boolean algebra.



1
Introduction

There has been burgeoning interest in probabilistic programming languages in the
last ten years and workshops on the topic now figure regularly as satellites of
both programming languages conferences and machine learning conferences. The
main motivation is building
compositional
models of probabilistic
processes and performing inference on them; see, for example, the thesis of Dan
Roy
[
25
]
or the papers on Church
[
16
]
and
Anglican
[
33
]
. For machine learning applications the notion of
conditioning is fundamental and the striking results of

[
1
,
2
]
show that this is a subtle issue.

The combination of probability and higher type programming has been both
technically challenging and important for the development of semantics for such
languages. There are a variety of approaches based on probabilistic coherence
spaces
[
9
]
or cones
[
10
]
, quasi-Borel
spaces
[
31
,
17
,
32
]
and Boolean-valued
models
[
3
]
which was based on Dana Scott’s vision
[
28
]
.
Stochastic lambda-calculi have appeared
[
6
,
7
,
8
]

with important contributions to the understanding of probability theory at
higher type. The work on quasi-Borel spaces gives a cartesian closed category
that can serve as the foundation for a typed higher-order probabilistic
programming language
[
17
]
.

In the work described in
[
3
]
a Boolean-valued domain theory was
developed. The idea is to use one of the standard set-theoretic models of the

\lambda
-calculus but interpreted in a suitable Boolean-valued universe of
sets. However, the basic domain theoretic definitions of directed set,
supremum, reflexive dcpo and continuity were interpreted as usual. The theory
presented there is rather complicated and had artificial restrictions on the way
randomness was handled. In the present paper a
completely
Boolean-valued
point of view is adopted and even basic concepts, like equality and order, are
all interpreted in a Boolean-valued logic. This leads to a much simpler theory
but still in line with the vision of
[
28
]
.

We show that untyped
\lambda
-calculus can be interpreted in domain-valued
random variables. In order to employ the theory of Boolean-valued sets, for most
of the article we actually use the Boolean-valued power set rather than random
variables, and then show that there is an isomorphism between the two at the
end. We focus on the reflexive domain construction rather than the language and
its semantics; it is the completely Boolean-valued reconstruction of domain
theory that we feel is the interesting contribution of the present paper. The
notion of equality has to be interpreted in the Boolean algebra and when we say
that an equation is valid in the model we mean that its interpretation is the
top element of the domain.

What makes the theory of Boolean-valued sets necessary is that domain-valued
random variables do not form a
continuous
dcpo. However, when
“continuous dcpo” is given its interpretation in Boolean-valued sets, they are.

The version of Boolean-valued sets we have used is the original version in terms
of a cumulative hierarchy. If the reader prefers, they may rephrase the
arguments results in terms of topos theory in a Boolean topos, and we outline
the connection of the two in Section
3
, though the only part
of this theory that we use is the ability to define functions.

As an example of how to apply Boolean-valued domain theory, we give a

\lambda
-calculus proof of the Kleene-Post theorem
[
20
, 2.2 Corollary
1]
that there are incomparable many-one degrees
1
1

1

We
formulated it in terms of many-one degrees for simplicity. It could also be
redone with Turing degrees instead.
, similar to Spector’s
[
30
, Theorem
2]
proof using measure theory.

There are a number of directions for future work. First, one can use this
construction to give semantics to a
\lambda
-calculus extended with
probabilistic choice as was done in
[
3
]
. In this model it will be
interesting to see which equations involving the interplay of choice and the
standard
\lambda
-calculus constructions are valid. One could then relate it
to an operational semantics as, for example, in
[
8
]
but one would
need a Boolean-valued notion of operational semantics. More interestingly one
could define conditioning as a primitive and explore its semantics.

A second line of research is the notion of approximation. A notion of
“approximate equality” has been developed recently
[
22
]
; the
connection to the notion of equality used in the present paper is unclear but
there is a similarity in that in both cases equality may only hold partially.
Whether this would prove to be a useful synthesis of the ideas remains to be
seen.

2
Background on Boolean-Valued Set Theory

In this section we describe how statements and proofs in ordinary set theory can
be re-interpreted in a Boolean-valued sense. This interpretation is originally
due to Dana Scott
[
26
]
and Robert Solovay, textbook
expositions can be found in
[
19
, Chapter 14]
and
[
5
]
. The reader who wishes to ignore set theory entirely and rephrase results using topos theory (taking responsibility for redoing the proofs) should skip to Section
3
.

Throughout this section we let
A
be an arbitrary complete Boolean algebra. The idea is that
A
will play the role that two-element Boolean algebra
2
plays in ordinary logic. We can build up the class of
A
-valued sets,
V^{A}
, by considering an
A
-valued set
X
to be a partial function that assigns to each element
x
of its domain the amount, valued in
A
, that
x
is an element of
X
. More accurately, we build up
V^{A}
by the following iterative construction, which generalizes von Neumann’s construction:

\displaystyle V^{A}_{0}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle V^{A}_{\alpha+1}

\displaystyle=\{f:V^{A}_{\alpha}\rightharpoonup A\}

\displaystyle V^{A}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}V^{A}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\text{for }\alpha\text{ a limit ordinal.}

Then either informally, or using proper classes,
V^{A}=\bigcup\limits_{\alpha\in\mathbf{Ord}}V^{A}_{\alpha}
.

We use the term
A
-valued set
for the elements of
V^{A}
, but we also will use the shorter term
A
-set
, and this helps to avoid certain opportunities for confusion arising from the term “valued”.

We can then interpret
\in
,
\subseteq
and
=
by the following mutually recursive formulas
2
2

2

We have used
\|{\mbox{-}}\|
instead of semantic brackets because we require the semantic brackets later on for interpreting
\lambda
-calculus.
:

\displaystyle\|x\in X\|

\displaystyle=\bigvee_{t\in\mathrm{dom}X}\|x=t\|\land X(t)

\displaystyle\|X\subseteq Y\|

\displaystyle=\bigwedge_{t\in\mathrm{dom}X}X(t)\Rightarrow\|t\in Y\|

\displaystyle\|x=y\|

\displaystyle=\|x\subseteq y\|\land\|y\subseteq x\|.

In the above, and all that follows, we use an operator precedence convention for
\bigvee
and
\bigwedge
that agrees with operator precedence for
\exists
and
\forall
, so there is an implicit bracket around everything to the right of such a join or meet.

Recall that the first-order language of set theory, which we write as
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbf{Set}}
, is the usual first-order language for a signature with equality and one two-place relation symbol, namely
\in
. We write
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbf{Set}}(V^{A})
for this language extended with constants from
V^{A}
.

In any Boolean algebra, we can interpret the connectives of propositional logic, and therefore any propositional formula. Using the completeness of
A
, we can interpret quantifiers by interpreting the universal quantifier as a meet and the existential quantifier as a join. All together, this gives us an interpretation of
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbf{Set}}(V^{A})
in
V^{A}
.

Meta-Theorem 2.1

(
V^{A}
as a model)
.

(i)

If
\phi\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbf{Set}}(V^{A})
is a theorem of ZFC set theory, then
\|\phi\|=1
in
V^{A}
.

(ii)

The inference rules of first-order logic can be applied to theorems of ZFC set theory and statements about elements of
V^{A}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbf{Set}}(V^{A})
.

(iii)

If
\|\exists x.\Phi(x,X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})\|=a
, where
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in V^{A}
(and we allow
n=0
), then there exists
X_{0}\in V^{A}
such that
\|\Phi(X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})\|=a
.

Proof.

For (i) and (ii), see
[
5
, Theorem 1.17]
; (iii) is a particular instance of what we shall later call the “completeness” of
V^{A}
- see
[
19
, Lemma 14.19]
or
[
5
, Lemma 1.27]
.
∎

It is helpful to make certain constructions of
A
-sets explicit. If
x,y\in V^{A}
, we can define the singleton
\{x\}^{A}
, unordered pair
\{x,y\}^{A}
and ordered pair
(x,y)^{A}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}\{x\}^{A}

\displaystyle=\{x\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(\{x,y\}^{A})

\displaystyle=\{x,y\}

\displaystyle\{x\}^{A}(x)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\{x,y\}^{A}(x)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\{x,y\}^{A}(y)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}((x,y)^{A})

\displaystyle=\{\{x\}^{A},\{x,y\}^{A}\}

\displaystyle(x,y)^{A}(\{x\}^{A})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle(x,y)^{A}(\{x,y\}^{A})

\displaystyle=1.

For
X,Y\in V^{A}
we define
X\mathrel{\times_{\!A}}Y
as follows.

\displaystyle X\mathrel{\times_{\!A}}Y

\displaystyle:\{(x,y)^{A}\mid x\in\mathrm{dom}(X),y\in\mathrm{dom}(Y)\}\rightarrow A

\displaystyle(X\mathrel{\times_{\!A}}Y)(x,y)^{A}

\displaystyle=\|x\in X\|\land\|y\in Y\|.

For each
A
-set
X
, the
A
-valued power set
\mathcal{P}^{A}(X)
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(\mathcal{P}^{A}(X))

\displaystyle=\{u\in V^{A}\mid\mathrm{dom}(u)=\mathrm{dom}(X)\text{ and }\forall t\in\mathrm{dom}(u).u(t)\leq X(t)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{A}(X)(u)

\displaystyle=1

For all
S,X\in V^{A}
we have:

\|S\in\mathcal{P}^{A}(X)\|=\|S\subseteq X\|,

which is how the power set axiom is proved for
V^{A}
.

If
\Phi
is a set-theoretic formula we can define the
A
-set

\displaystyle\{x\in X\mid\Phi(x)\}^{A}

\displaystyle:\mathrm{dom}(X)\rightarrow A

\displaystyle\{x\in X\mid\Phi(x)\}^{A}(t)

\displaystyle=X(t)\land\|\Phi(t)\|,

and this is how the axiom of separation is proved for
V^{A}
.

If we now consider von Neumann’s universe
V
of classic set theory constructed inductively on ordinals
\alpha\in\mathbf{Ord}
, then for each set
S\in V_{\alpha}
, there is a corresponding element
\check{S}\in V^{A}_{\alpha}
defined recursively. The domain of
\check{S}
is
\{\check{x}\mid x\in S\}
, and it is defined by:

\displaystyle\check{\emptyset}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\check{S}(\check{x})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\text{for all }x\in S.

To describe how a statement in set theory about an ordinary set in
X\in V
translates to a statement about
\check{X}\in V^{A}
, we need the notion of a
\Delta_{0}
statement. Bounded quantifiers are those of the form
\forall x\in X.\Phi(x)
and
\exists x\in X.\Psi(x)
,
i.e.
in the language of set theory
\forall x.x\in X\Rightarrow\Phi(x)
and
\exists x.x\in X\land\Psi(x)
. A
\Delta_{0}
formula
\Phi\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbf{Set}}(V^{A})
is one containing only bounded quantifiers. We have
\Delta_{0}
invariance, see
[
19
, Lemma 14.21]
or
[
5
, Theorem 1.23 (v)]
.
3
3

3

Note that Bell uses the alternative terminology “restricted” for
\Delta_{0}
.

Meta-Theorem 2.2

(
\Delta_{0}
invariance)
.

Let
\Phi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be a
\Delta_{0}
-formula, with free variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in V
. Then

\Phi(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\Leftrightarrow\|\Phi(\check{X}_{1},\ldots,\check{X}_{n})\|=1

∎

We point out some useful consequences of Meta-Theorem
2.2
. The statements “
S
is a singleton whose only element is
x
”, “
S
is an unordered pair of
x
and
y
” and “
S
is an ordered pair, first element
x
, second element
y
” are all
\Delta_{0}
statements, and so the following all hold

\{x\}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt

\displaystyle=\{\check{x}\}^{A}

\{x,y\}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt

\displaystyle=\{\check{x},\check{y}\}^{A}

(x,y)

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt

\displaystyle=(\check{x},\check{y})^{A}.

Another important consequence of Meta-Theorem
2.2
is that
\check{\omega}
is the smallest inductive
4
4

4

In the sense used to formulate the axiom of infinity in ZF.

A
-set in
V^{A}
, so that
\check{\omega}
is the
\omega
of
V^{A}
. Given any set
X
, we say a subset
S\subseteq X
is finite if there exists
n\in\omega
and
f:n\rightarrow X
such that
S=\mathrm{im}(f)
. We write
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
for the set of all finite subsets of
X
. If we interpret this with
A
-sets, for each
X\in V^{A}
we can define
\mathcal{P}^{A}_{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
to be the set of finite
A
-subsets of
X
, using the axiom of separation. The following proposition is a consequence of the distributive law - see
[
21
, 3.1.11]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

For all sets
X
:

\|\mathcal{P}^{A}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\check{X})=\hbox{\hskip 441.65944pt\hskip-16.65628pt\hbox{$\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(X)$}\hskip-16.65628pt\hskip-441.65944pt\raisebox{18.50012pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{52312104}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-441.65944pt\hskip 441.65944pt}\|=1

∎

We can create categories
\mathbf{Rel}_{A}
and
\mathbf{Set}_{A}
from
V^{A}
as follows. Each has
V^{A}
as its class of objects. For each
X,Y\in V^{A}
, the set of binary relations can be defined as
\mathrm{dom}(\mathcal{P}^{A}(X\mathrel{\times_{\!A}}Y))
, and the set of functions
\{X\overset{A}{\rightarrow}Y\}\in V^{A}
can be defined using products and the axiom of separation. We define:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Rel}_{A}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dom}(\mathcal{P}^{A}(X\mathrel{\times_{\!A}}Y))/\sim

\displaystyle\mathbf{Set}_{A}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathrm{dom}(\{X\overset{A}{\rightarrow}Y\})\mid\|f\in\{X\overset{A}{\rightarrow}Y\}\|=1\}/\sim,

where
\sim
is the equivalence relation defined by
f\sim g
iff
\|f=g\|=1
. Identity elements are defined as identity relations, and composition by composition of relations, which is well-defined with respect to
\sim
.

3
Boolean-Valued Setoids

We now discuss the kind of “Boolean-valued sets” that are essentially
A
-valued models of the theory of
=
. These can be considered an alternative description of either sheaves or separated presheaves on
A
, and are usually discussed in the more general case where
A
is a Heyting algebra, such as in
[
12
]
,
[
15
, §11.9]
and
[
23
, Definition 5.1]
. Although we will use only one definition of
A
-valued sets, the distinction between
A
-valued relations that satisfy the definition of a function
internally
and functions that do so gives us two distinct categories.

In order to distinguish the
A
-valued sets we will be discussing from elements of
V^{A}
, we call them
A
-valued setoids
, or simply
A
-setoids
, because
setoid
is a long-established term for a set equipped with an equivalence relation.
An
A
-setoid is a pair
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
where
\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X}:X\times X\rightarrow A
. The two axioms of
\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X}
are symmetry and transitivity,
i.e.

\displaystyle\forall x,y\in X.\|x=y\|_{X}

\displaystyle=\|y=x\|_{X}

\displaystyle\forall x,y,z\in X.\|x=y\|_{X}\land\|y=z\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\|x=z\|_{X}.

so
A
-valued setoids are “
A
-valued
partial
equivalence relations”.

Since reflexivity is not assumed, the statement
\|x=x\|_{X}
does not represent a tautology, but its intended interpretation is, in some sense, the degree to which
x\in X
, or the place where
x\in X
. So we define the notation
\varepsilon_{X}(x)=\|x=x\|_{X}
.

We say
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
is
total
if
\|x=x\|_{X}=1
for all
x\in X
,
i.e.
if reflexivity holds; it is
strict
if
\|x=y\|_{X}=1
implies
x=y
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
be an
A
-setoid. We say it is
complete
5
5

5

This definition is slightly different from that used in
[
12
, 4.10,4.11]
and elsewhere, which would not suit us because under that definition
\mathcal{P}^{A}(X)
is not complete, when considered as an
A
-setoid in the sense we will define in Definition
3.13
.
if either, hence both, of the following equivalent properties holds of it:

(i)

For any family of elements
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in
A
and corresponding family
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
in
X
such that for all
i,j\in I
,
a_{i}\land a_{j}\leq\|x_{i}=x_{j}\|_{X}
, there exists
x\in X
such that for all
i\in I
,
a_{i}\leq\|x_{i}=x\|_{X}
.

(ii)

For any pairwise disjoint family of elements
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in
A
and corresponding family
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
in
X
such that for all
i\in I
,
a_{i}\leq\|x_{i}=x_{i}\|_{X}
, there exists
x\in X
such that for all
i\in I
,
a_{i}\leq\|x_{i}=x\|_{X}
. ∎

Definition 3.2
.

If
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
is an
A
-setoid and
(Y,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{Y})
is a set and a function
\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{Y}:Y\times Y\rightarrow A
, and
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a bijection such that for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
:

\|f(x_{1})=f(x_{2})\|_{Y}=\|x_{1}=x_{2}\|_{X},

then we say
f
is a
strict isomorphism
, and it follows that
(Y,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{Y})
is an
A
-setoid, and is total, strict or complete iff
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
is. ∎

Definition 3.3
.

The product
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})\times(Y,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{Y})
is defined to have

\|(x,y)=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\|_{X\times Y}=\|x=x^{\prime}\|_{X}\land\|y=y^{\prime}\|_{Y}.

∎

Definition 3.4
.

A
predicate
on
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
is a function
S:X\rightarrow A
such that:

(i)

For all
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
,
\|x_{1}=x_{2}\|_{X}\leq S(x_{1})\Leftrightarrow S(x_{2})
.

(ii)

For all
x\in X
,
S(x)\leq\varepsilon_{X}(x)
. ∎

A binary relation
X\rightarrow Y
is simply a predicate on
X\times Y
.

We can now define the first two categories of
A
-setoids, the category of
A
-setoids with relations
\mathbf{SetoidRel}_{A}
, and the category of
A
-setoids with “relational functions”,
\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}
.

Definition 3.5
.

The categories
\mathbf{SetoidRel}_{A}
and
\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}
have
A
-setoids as objects. The hom set
\mathbf{SetoidRel}_{A}(X,Y)
is the set of binary relations
X\rightarrow Y
. In this case there is no need to take a quotient by any equivalence relation.

The identity relation
\mathrm{id}_{X}:X\rightarrow X
is defined by:

\mathrm{id}_{X}(x_{1},x_{2})=\|x_{1}=x_{2}\|_{X},

composition of relations
R:X\rightarrow Y
and
S:Y\rightarrow Z
is defined by

(S\circ R)(x,z)=\bigvee_{y\in Y}S(y,z)\land R(x,y).

The category
\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}
has the same objects, identity maps, definition of composition and equivalence relation on morphisms, but the a morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
must satisfy the additional conditions:

(iii)

For all
x\in X
,
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
,
f(x,y_{1})\land f(x,y_{2})\leq\|y_{1}=y_{2}\|_{Y}
.

(iv)

For all
x\in X
,
\varepsilon_{X}(x)\leq\bigvee_{y\in Y}f(x,y)
. ∎

We define the category of
A
-setoids and “functional functions”,
\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}
, as follows.

Definition 3.6
.

The category
\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}
has
A
-setoids as objects. The morphisms are defined starting with an
A
-setoid:

\displaystyle X\multimap Y

\displaystyle=\{f:X\rightarrow Y\mid\forall x_{1},x_{2}\in X.\|x_{1}=x_{2}\|_{X}\leq\|f(x_{1})=f(x_{2})\|_{Y}\}

\displaystyle\|f_{1}=f_{2}\|_{X\multimap Y}

\displaystyle=\bigwedge_{x\in X}\varepsilon_{X}(x)\Rightarrow\|f_{1}(x)=f_{2}(x)\|_{Y}.

Then the hom set
\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}(X,Y)
is the quotient set of this,
6
6

6

Monro calls these
admissible functions
in
[
23
, Definition 5.2]
.

i.e.

X\multimap Y
modulo the equivalence relation:

f_{1}\sim f_{2}\text{ iff }\forall x\in X.\varepsilon_{X}(x)\leq\|f_{1}(x)=f_{2}(x)\|_{Y}.

Identity maps and composition are defined as for functions. ∎

The category
\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}
is called
\mathbf{Mod}_{0}(A)
in
[
23
, Definition 5.2]
, where it is proved to be a quasi-topos. The full subcategory on non-empty
7
7

7

We mean not having
\emptyset
as an underlying set. It is still allowed, and necessary, to have sets that do not assign any value other than
0
to any element, which are “empty” in terms of Boolean-valued logic.
complete
A
-setoids is a topos
[
23
, Proposition 5.6 (ii)]
equivalent to the category of sheaves on the canonical topology on
A
.

By definition,
\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}
is a subcategory of
\mathbf{SetoidRel}_{A}
. We now show that mapping a functional function to its graph defines a faithful functor from
\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}
.

Definition 3.7
.

The following defines a faithful functor
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}
. On objects,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})=(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
. For
f\in\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}(X,Y)
, we define

\boldsymbol{\gamma}(f)(x,y)=\varepsilon_{X}(x)\land\|f(x)=y\|_{Y}.

∎

We can formulate a notion of poset that does not require us to define
\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X}
but can instead be defined in terms of it. It turns out to be useful in the general theory, as well as for dealing with posets that are
A
-setoids or
A
-sets.

Definition 3.8
.

An
A
-poset
is a pair
(X,\|\mbox{$\leq$}\|_{X})
, where
X
is a set and
\|\mbox{$\leq$}\|_{X}:X\times X\rightarrow A
is such that for all
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in X
:

(i)

\|x_{1}\leq x_{2}\|_{X}\land\|x_{2}\leq x_{3}\|_{X}\leq\|x_{1}\leq x_{3}\|_{X}.

(ii)

\|x_{1}\leq x_{2}\|_{X}\leq\|x_{1}\leq x_{1}\|_{X}\land\|x_{2}\leq x_{2}\|_{X}.

We then define
\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X}
by

\|x_{1}=x_{2}\|_{X}=\|x_{1}\leq x_{2}\|_{X}\land\|x_{2}\leq x_{1}\|_{X}.

(3.9)

Then
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
is an
A
-setoid, and
\|\mbox{$\leq$}\|_{X}\in\mathbf{SetoidRel}_{A}(X,X)
is a reflexive, antisymmetric, transitive relation when these statements are interpreted in the
A
-valued sense.
If
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
is an
A
-setoid and
\|\mbox{$\leq$}\|_{X}\in\mathbf{SetoidRel}_{A}(X,X)
is a relation that is reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive, in the
A
-valued sense, then
(X,\|\mbox{$\leq$}\|_{X})
is an
A
-poset in the above sense, and
\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X}
satisfies (
3.9
). ∎

Since
\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X}
is defined in terms of
\|\mbox{$\leq$}\|_{X}
, the following fact is convenient for proving that a function on underlying sets
f:X\rightarrow Y
not only defines an element of
\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}(X,Y)
but also a monotone function in the
A
-valued sense.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
(X,\|\mbox{$\leq$}\|_{X}),(Y,\|\mbox{$\leq$}\|_{Y})
be
A
-posets, and suppose that
f:X\rightarrow Y
is
A
-monotone in the sense that for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in X

\|x_{1}\leq x_{2}\|_{X}\leq\|f(x_{1})\leq f(x_{2})\|_{Y}.

(This is monotonicity using the interpretation of logic in Section
2
.) Then
f\in X\multimap Y
. Every
f\in\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}(X,Y)
that is monotone in the
A
-valued interpretation of the term is of this form. ∎

The following definition and theorem are based on
[
24
, Proposition 3.3]
.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
(X,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X}),(Y,\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
be
A
-setoids such that
Y
is complete, and let
f\in\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}(X,Y)
. For each
x\in X
, there exists
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}(f)(x)\in Y
such that

f(x,y)\leq\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}(f)(x)=y\|_{Y}.

(3.12)

Any choice of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}(f)(x)
for all
x\in X
defines an element
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}(f)\in\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}(X,Y)
such that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}(f))=f
, and in fact this establishes a
\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}
isomorphism
\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}(X,Y)\cong\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}(X,Y)
. ∎

Every
A
-set defines an
A
-setoid as follows.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
X\in V^{A}
. Define
\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X}:\mathrm{dom}(X)\times\mathrm{dom}(X)\rightarrow A
by

\|x=y\|_{X}=\|x\in X\|\land\|y\in X\|\land\|x=y\|

(3.14)

Then
(\mathrm{dom}(X),\|\mbox{$=$}\|_{X})
is an
A
-setoid, which we write
\mathrm{Oid}(X)
when we need to be unambiguous. ∎

Definition 3.15
.

Let
X\in V^{A}
. For each
S\in V^{A}
such that
\|S\subseteq X\|=1
, then
e(S)
, defined as follows, is a predicate on
\mathrm{Oid}(X)
:

e(S)(x)=\|x\in S\|,

where
x\in\mathrm{Oid}(X)
. In particular, this holds if
S\in\mathrm{dom}(\mathcal{P}^{A}(X))
. ∎

Definition 3.16
.

The following defines
\mathrm{Oid}:\mathbf{Rel}_{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{SetoidRel}_{A}
and
\mathrm{Oid}:\mathbf{Set}_{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}
as functors, where
f:X\rightarrow Y
in either
\mathbf{Rel}_{A}
or
\mathbf{Set}_{A}
:

\mathrm{Oid}(f)(x,y)=\|(x,y)^{A}\in f\|

These functors are full, faithful and essentially surjective, and therefore define equivalences
\mathbf{Rel}_{A}\simeq\mathbf{SetoidRel}_{A}
and
\mathbf{Set}_{A}\simeq\mathbf{SetoidR}_{A}
. ∎

Theorem 3.17
.

For each
X\in V^{A}
,
\mathrm{Oid}(\mathcal{P}^{A}(X))
is complete. Moreover, if
\Phi
is a formula of
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbf{Set}}(V^{A})
and
\|\exists S\in\mathcal{P}^{A}(X).\Phi(S,\ldots)\|=a\in A
, then there exists
S\in\mathrm{Oid}(\mathcal{P}^{A}(X))
such that
\|\Phi(S,\ldots)\|=a
. ∎

Corollary 3.18
.

For each
X,Y\in V^{A}
,
\mathrm{Oid}(\mathcal{P}^{A}(X\mathrel{\times_{\!A}}Y))
and
\mathrm{Oid}(\{X\overset{A}{\rightarrow}Y\})
are complete, and if we prove a relation or a function exists in the Boolean-valued sense, satisfying some formula
\Phi\in\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbf{Set}}(V^{A})
, then there exists a corresponding element of
\mathrm{Oid}(\mathcal{P}^{A}(X\mathrel{\times_{\!A}}Y))
or
\mathrm{Oid}(\{X\overset{A}{\rightarrow}Y\})
satisfying
\Phi
. ∎

4
Models of Untyped
\lambda
-calculus in
A
-sets

The purpose of this section is to show that we can model untyped
\lambda
-calculus in
A
-valued sets. We use the Engeler model
[
11
]
as the main example. We will repeatedly use Meta-Theorem
2.1
to prove statements in
V^{A}
by proving them in set theory first. The reader who would prefer to use a topos formulation might prefer to use the categorical formulation of the Engeler model from
[
18
]
.

4.1
Background on Domain Theory

A
dcpo

(D,\leq)
is a poset that is directed complete,
i.e.
such that every directed set has a supremum. Every complete lattice is a dcpo. The least element of a dcpo
(D,\leq)
, if it exists, is called the
bottom element
and is written
\bot
. In a dcpo
D
, where
d,e\in D
, we say that
d
is
way below

e
, written
d\ll e
, if for each directed set
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
e\leq\bigvee_{i\in I}e_{i}
, there exists some
j\in I
such that
d\leq e_{j}
. The relation
\ll
is transitive and antisymmetric, but in general is neither reflexive nor irreflexive.
We write
\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\twoheadrightarrow$}d=\{e\in D\mid e\ll d\}
. A dcpo
D
is called
continuous
if for all
d\in D
, the set
\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\twoheadrightarrow$}d
is directed and
d=\bigvee\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\twoheadrightarrow$}d
. A continuous dcpo is called a
domain
. A complete lattice that is continuous as a dcpo is called a
complete lattice
.
A
basis
or
base
for a domain
D
is a subset
B\subseteq D
such that for all
d\in D
,
\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\twoheadrightarrow$}d\cap B
is directed, and
d=\bigvee(\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\twoheadrightarrow$}d\cap B)
. It follows that if
D
is continuous, then
D
is a base for
D
. If
D
has a countable base, we say it is a
countably-based domain
. See
[
14
]
for more related concepts.

If
D
and
E
are dcpos, a function
f:D\rightarrow E
is said to be
Scott continuous
if it is monotone and preserves directed suprema. We write
{[D\rightarrow E]}
for the set of Scott-continuous maps
D\rightarrow E
. This is a dcpo when given the pointwise ordering, and directed suprema are calculated pointwise. When we refer to this as a dcpo, we always use this structure.
The
Scott topology
on a dcpo
D
is the topology whose open sets are the sets
U\subseteq D
such that
U
is an up set and for all directed sets
(d_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
\bigvee_{i\in I}d_{i}\in U
there exists
i\in I
such that
d_{i}\in U
. If
D
and
E
are dcpos, a function
f:D\rightarrow E
is Scott continuous iff it is a continuous map from
D
to
E
equipped with their respective Scott topologies.

Definition 4.1
.

A
reflexive dcpo

[
4
, Definition 5.4.1]
is a triple
(D,\mathbf{fun},\mathbf{lam})
, where
D
is a dcpo with bottom,
8
8

8

This is what is meant by a cpo in
[
4
, Definition 1.2.1 (ii)]
.
and
\mathbf{fun}:D\rightarrow{[D\rightarrow D]}
and
\mathbf{lam}:{[D\rightarrow D]}\rightarrow D
are Scott-continuous maps making
{[D\rightarrow D]}
a retract of
D
,
i.e.

\mathbf{fun}\circ\mathbf{lam}=\mathrm{id}_{{[D\rightarrow D]}}
. A reflexive dcpo is
extensional
iff
\mathbf{fun}
, or equivalently
\mathbf{lam}
is an isomorphism,
i.e.
iff additionally
\mathbf{lam}\circ\mathbf{fun}=\mathrm{id}_{D}
. ∎

It is often convenient to formulate this notion using the uncurried form of
\mathbf{fun}
, which is to say, we can equivalently define a reflexive dcpo to be a triple
(D,\cdot,\mathbf{lam})
where
D
is a dcpo with bottom, and
\cdot:D\times D\rightarrow D
and
\mathbf{lam}:{[D\rightarrow D]}\rightarrow D
are Scott-continuous maps such that for each
f\in{[D\rightarrow D]}
and
d\in D
we have
\mathbf{lam}(f)\cdot d=f(d)
.

To finish this subsection, we describe how the language of untyped
\lambda
-calculus can be interpreted in a reflexive dcpo. Given a reflexive dcpo
(D,\cdot,\mathbf{lam})
, and a set of variables
\mathrm{Var}
, a
valuation
is a partial function
\rho:\mathrm{Var}\rightarrow D
. We also choose a set of constants
\mathfrak{K}\subseteq D
, which is allowed to be any subset, including
\emptyset
and
D
itself. We then define the language of
\lambda
-calculus
\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})
as follows

Definition 4.2
.

The language
\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})
is defined inductively by the rules

x\in\mathrm{Var}
x\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})

d\in\mathfrak{K}
d\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})

x\in\mathrm{Var}
M\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})
\lambda x.M\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})

M\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})
N\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})
MN\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})

∎

We write
\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})
for
\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\emptyset)
, because this does not depend on
D
. The elements of
\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})
are called
pure
\lambda
-terms
. We write
\mathrm{fv}(M)
for the set of free variables of
M
, defined as usual.

We make the following observation about how the above definition is formulated in set theory.
9
9

9

This is needed to make the proof of Proposition
4.4
and later results that deal with viewing the syntax of
\lambda
-calculus from inside
V^{A}
more comprehensible.

Example 4.3
.

There exist
\Delta_{0}
formulas
{\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})}\text{-}\mathrm{ind}
and
{\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})}\text{-}\mathrm{ind}
, such that the sets
\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})
and
\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})
are respectively definable as the smallest sets satisfying their corresponding formula.

Proof.

To define
{\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})}\text{-}\mathrm{ind}
and
{\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})}\text{-}\mathrm{ind}
, we need to choose some way of representing the syntax of
\lambda
-calculus. For example, we could take the ordinals
0
to mean a variable,
1
a
\lambda
-abstraction,
2
an application and
3
a constant, so that a variable appears as
(0,x)
for
x\in\mathrm{Var}
, a
\lambda
-abstraction as
(1,(x,M))
where
x\in\mathrm{Var}
and
M
is a
\lambda
-term, an application as
(2,(M,N))
where
M,N
are
\lambda
-terms, and a constant as
(4,d)
where
d\in\mathfrak{K}
.

Then we can define:

\displaystyle{\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})}\text{-}\mathrm{ind}(X)

\displaystyle=(\forall x\in\mathrm{Var}.\exists p\in X.p=(0,x))

\displaystyle\land(\forall x\in\mathrm{Var}.\forall M\in X.\exists p\in X.p=(1,(x,M)))

\displaystyle\land(\forall M\in X.\forall N\in X.\exists p\in X.p=(2,(M,N))).

Strictly speaking this is not quite a
\Delta_{0}
formula, but all that needs to be done is to show that the statements
p=(0,x)
,
p=(1,(x,M))
and
p=(2,(M,N))
are expressible as
\Delta_{0}
formulas (with the usual set-theoretic representations of ordered pairs), which is left as an exercise to the reader. Then we can also define

{\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})}\text{-}\mathrm{ind}(X)={\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})}\text{-}\mathrm{ind}(X)\land(\forall d\in\mathfrak{K}.\exists p\in X.p=(3,d))

It is then clear that
\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})
and
\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})
are the least (with respect to
\subseteq
) sets satisfying their respective formulas.
∎

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\mathrm{Var}
be a set.

10

Not Boolean-valued, just the usual kind of set.
For any complete Boolean algebra
A

2.1
(i) and (iii) there exists

in the Boolean-valued sense,
i.e.

Base case for a variable:

If
(0,x)\in\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})
where
x\in\mathrm{Var}
, we start with the observation that
\hbox{\hskip . Since
\|{\Lambda(\hbox{\hskip \|(0^{A},\check{x})^{A}\in X\|=1
, and therefore
\|\hbox{\hskip \lambda
-abstraction:

such that

\|\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-24.40276pt\hbox{$(2,(M,N))$}\hskip-24.40276pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{18.50012pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}\in X\|=1
.

This completes the induction.
∎

The equational theory of
\lambda
-calculus, called
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is described following
[
4
]
:

Definition 4.5
.

Sentences of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
are of the form
M=N
, where
M,N\in\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})
. The theory
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is generated by the following rules, under modus ponens and
\alpha
-conversion.

(i)

(\lambda x.M)N=M[x:=N]
, where
M[x:=N]
is capture-avoiding substitution of
N
for
x
.

(ii)

M=M
.

(iii)

M=N\Rightarrow N=M
.

(iv)

M=N,N=L\Rightarrow M=L
.

(v)

M=N\Rightarrow MZ=NZ
.

(vi)

M=N\Rightarrow ZM=ZN
.

(vii)

M=N\Rightarrow\lambda x.M=\lambda x.N
. ∎

We write
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash M=N
to say that the equation
M=N
is derivable in the theory
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.

Example 4.6
.

Here is a brief description of the set-theoretic formulation of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. We can simply consider it to be a subset of
\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})\times\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})
, and interpret
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash M=N
as
(M,N)\in\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. We can define a formula
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\text{-}\mathrm{ind}(S)
, which we no longer require to be
\Delta_{0}
, that encodes the rules of forming
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
from Definition
4.5
, and then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
can be characterized as the least set (with respect to
\subseteq
) such that
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\text{-}\mathrm{ind}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
. This allows us to define
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{A}
in
V^{A}
.

We can then prove that
\|\check{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\subseteq\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{A}\|=1
by induction on the structure of an element of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, as was done to prove
\|\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-15.1528pt\hbox{$\Lambda(\mathrm{Var})$}\hskip-15.1528pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{18.50012pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}\subseteq\Lambda(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-7.79167pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Var}$}\hskip-7.79167pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt})^{A}\|=1
in Proposition
4.4
. ∎

Definition 4.7
.

Following
[
4
, Definition 5.4.2]
, we define the interpretation of
\lambda
-terms from
M\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})
in
D
, using a valuation
\rho
such that
\mathrm{fv}(M)\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(\rho)
, by

\displaystyle\llbracket{x}\rrbracket_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\rho(x)

\displaystyle x\in\mathrm{Var}

\displaystyle\llbracket{k}\rrbracket_{\rho}

\displaystyle=k

\displaystyle k\in\mathfrak{K}

\displaystyle\llbracket{MN}\rrbracket_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\rho}\cdot\llbracket{N}\rrbracket_{\rho}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\lambda x.M}\rrbracket_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{lam}(\bblambda d.\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\rho(x:=d)}),

where
\bblambda
is the “meta-lambda”, and
\rho(x:=d)
is the partial function where
\mathrm{dom}(\rho(x:=d))=\mathrm{dom}(\rho)\cup\{x\}
and

\rho(x:=d)(y)=\begin{cases}\rho(y)&\text{if }y\in\mathrm{dom}(\rho)\setminus\{x\}\\
d&\text{if }y=x\end{cases}

This is proved to define a model of
\lambda
-calculus in
[
4
, Theorem 5.4.4]
, which is to say, that for all
M,N\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var})
, if
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash M=N
, then for all valuations
\rho
,
\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\rho}=\llbracket{N}\rrbracket_{\rho}
. For closed terms
M\in\Lambda(D,\mathrm{Var},\mathfrak{K})
, we write
\llbracket{M}\rrbracket
for
\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\emptyset}
. ∎

We now give the Boolean-valued version.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
D\in V^{A}
be strict, total and complete as an
A
-setoid, and let there be
\mathbf{lam}
and
\cdot
in
V^{A}
such that
\|(D,\mathbf{lam},\cdot)\text{ is a reflexive dcpo}\|=1
. Take
\mathrm{Var}
to be a set of variables and
\mathfrak{K}\in V^{A}
such that
\|\mathfrak{K}\subseteq D\|=1
an
A
-set of constants. Then for each
\rho
that defines a partial function from

\mathrm{Var}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt

to

, there are functions

and
\llbracket{{\mbox{-}}}\rrbracket^{A}_{\rho}\in\mathbf{SetoidF}_{A}(\hbox{\hskip and also

and

.

From this, we get that if
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash M=N
, then
\|\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-15.79164pt\hbox{$(M,N)$}\hskip-15.79164pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{18.50012pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox 451.29065pt}\in\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{A}\|=1
, and therefore for all
\rho
,
\|\llbracket{\check{M}}\rrbracket_{\rho}=\llbracket{\check{N}}\rrbracket_{\rho}\|=1
. Since
D
is strict, this implies that actually
\llbracket{\check{M}}\rrbracket_{\rho}=\llbracket{\check{N}}\rrbracket_{\rho}
in the usual sense as well.
∎

4.2
The Engeler Model

We start with a basic result about the power set.

Proposition 4.9
.

For any set
X
, the power set
\mathcal{P}(X)
, when ordered using the subset relation
\subseteq
, is a dcpo (in fact, a complete lattice). If
S,T\in\mathcal{P}(X)
,
S\ll T
iff
S
is finite and
S\subseteq T
. The set of finite sets
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
is a base, and if
X
is countable, it is a countable base.

We can then apply this in
V^{A}
as follows.

Proposition 4.10
.

For any
X\in V^{A}
, the
A
-set
\mathcal{P}^{A}(X)
, ordered with the subset relation, is a continuous lattice with base
\mathcal{P}^{A}_{\mathrm{fin}}(X)
, interpreted in the
A
-valued sense. As an
A
-setoid,
\mathcal{P}^{A}(X)
is complete and total. If
X=\check{Y}
, then

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(Y)

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt

is a base, and
\mathcal{P}^{A}(\check{Y})
is strict.

Proof.

We get the first part by applying Meta-Theorem
2.1
to Proposition
4.9
. By Theorem
3.17
,
\mathcal{P}^{A}(X)
is complete, and it is clear from its definition that it is total. Now, if
X=\check{Y}
, we use the fact that
\|\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-16.13547pt\hbox{$\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(Y)$}\hskip-16.13547pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{18.50012pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}=\mathcal{P}^{A}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\check{Y})\|=1
(Proposition
2.3
), so

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(Y)

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt

is also a base for
\mathcal{P}^{A}(\check{Y})
with Boolean value 1.

Finally, to show that
\mathcal{P}^{A}(\check{Y})
is strict, let
S,T\in\mathcal{P}^{A}(\check{Y})
such that
\|S=T\|_{\mathcal{P}^{A}(\check{Y})}=1
, which is equivalent to
\|S=T\|=1
. First observe that
\mathrm{dom}(S)=\mathrm{dom}(\check{Y})=\mathrm{dom}(T)
, and then that for all
\check{y}\in\mathrm{dom}(\check{Y})
, we have
\|\check{y}\in S\|=S(\check{y})
and likewise for
T
. Since
\|S=T\|=1
, for all
y\in Y
we have

S(\check{y})=\|\check{y}\in S\|=\|\check{y}\in T\|=T(\check{y}),

so
S=T
.
∎

As in
[
11
]
and
[
4
, Definition 5.4.5]
we can define the Engeler model, an adaptation of the Scott-Plotkin graph model that uses set-theoretic operations instead of Gödel numbering. We define it as follows.
11
11

11

What we really need of
E
is for it to be countable, non-empty, and an algebra of the functor
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathrm{Id}
, such that the structure map
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(E)\times E\rightarrow E
is injective, which implies
E
must be infinite. We cannot use the initial algebra of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathrm{Id}
, because it is
\emptyset
.

Let
E_{0}=\{\emptyset\}
. We define
E_{n+1}=(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(E_{n})\times E_{n})\uplus E_{n}
and
E=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}E_{n}
. Then we have a map
({\mbox{-}},{\mbox{-}}):\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(E)\times E\rightarrow E
defined by pairing, since for each
S\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(E)
we have
S\subseteq E_{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For reasons that will become clear later, we express the existence of the Engeler model in the following manner.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
E
be a set equipped with an injective mapping
({\mbox{-}},{\mbox{-}}):\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(E)\times E\rightarrow E
. Then if we define
\mathbf{lam}:{[\mathcal{P}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(E)]}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(E)
and
{\mbox{-}}\cdot{\mbox{-}}
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{lam}(f)

\displaystyle=\{(K,q)\mid q\in f(K)\}=\{x\in E\mid\exists K\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(E),q\in f(K).x=(K,q)\}

\displaystyle F\cdot X

\displaystyle=\{q\in E\mid\exists K\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(E).K\subseteq X\text{ and }(K,q)\in F\},

where
f\in{[\mathcal{P}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(E)]}
, and
F,X\in\mathcal{P}(E)
. Then
(\mathcal{P}(E),\cdot,\mathbf{lam})
is a reflexive dcpo.

We omit the proof as it is essentially proved in the references already given.

We can now build the Engeler model in
V^{A}
, as follows.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
E
be the set defined before Proposition
4.11
.
(\mathcal{P}^{A}(\check{E}),\|\subseteq\|,\cdot,\mathbf{lam})
is an
A
-valued reflexive dcpo, where
\cdot
and
\mathbf{lam}
are defined by the
A
-valued interpretation of the definitions given in Proposition
4.11
.

Proof.

By Meta-Theorem
2.2
,

({\mbox{-}},{\mbox{-}})

defines an injective function

, it also defines an injective function

By combining Theorem
4.12
with Proposition
4.10
, we can apply Theorem
4.8
to obtain a function
\llbracket{{\mbox{-}}}\rrbracket^{A}
that interprets
\lambda
-terms in a manner that respects provable equations between them.

The following lemma shows that if we only use pure
\lambda
-terms we do not get anything new by using
\mathcal{P}^{A}(\check{E})
instead of
\mathcal{P}(E)
as a model of
\lambda
-calculus.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let

and

a valuation such that

, and consider

and

as defined for

and

respectively. Then

\|\llbracket{\check{M}}\rrbracket^{A}_{\check{\rho}}=\hbox{\hskip In particular, since

, if

\|\llbracket{\check{M}}\rrbracket^{A}=\hbox{\hskip Proof.

We prove it by induction on the structure of

valuations
\rho

•

with

and

•

Inductive step for a
\lambda
-abstraction:

For this step, we first point out that when interpreting
\llbracket{\lambda x.M}\rrbracket_{\rho}
in the Engeler model, we can simplify the expression

\displaystyle\mathbf{lam}(\bblambda d.\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\rho(x:=d)})

\displaystyle=\{(K,q)\in E\mid q\in\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{\rho(x:=K)}\}

So for
p\in E
we have:

\displaystyle\|\check{p}\in\llbracket{\lambda\check{x}.\check{M}}\rrbracket^{A}_{\check{\rho}}\|

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\|\exists K\in\mathcal{P}^{A}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\check{E}),q\in\check{E}.\check{p}=(K,q)\text{ and }q\in\llbracket{\check{M}}\rrbracket^{A}_{\check{\rho}(\check{x}:=K)}\|

\displaystyle={}

∎

4.3
Injective Spaces and Oracles

We can encode the Booleans
\{\bot,\top\}
, and natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
in
\lambda
-calculus in several ways, such as with the Church numerals. The details of the encoding do not matter, only the following.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
(D,\mathbf{fun},\mathbf{lam})
be a reflexive dcpo,
\bot,\top
be closed
\lambda
-terms, and
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of closed
\lambda
-terms. Then
(D,\mathbf{fun},\mathbf{lam},\bot,\top,(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})
is a
reflexive dcpo with numerals
if

(i)

\llbracket{\bot}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket{\top}\rrbracket
are distinct elements of
D
.

(ii)

There exists a closed
\lambda
-term
\mathbf{if}
such that for all
\lambda
-terms
M,N
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash\mathbf{if}\top MN=M
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash\mathbf{if}\bot MN=N
.

(iii)

There exist closed
\lambda
-terms
\mathbf{succ}
and
\mathbf{pred}
representing the successor and predecessor operations on
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
i.e.
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash\mathbf{succ}c_{n}=c_{n+1}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash\mathbf{pred}c_{n+1}=c_{n}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash\mathbf{pred}c_{0}=c_{0}
.

(iv)

There exists a closed
\lambda
-term
\mathbf{0}^{?}
such that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash\mathbf{0}^{?}c_{0}=\top
and for all
n>0
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash\mathbf{0}^{?}c_{n}=\bot
.

Proposition 4.16
.

The Church Booleans and Church numerals make the Engeler model into a reflexive continuous lattice with numerals.

The proof is a standard working out of basic facts about Church numerals and Booleans.

Corollary 4.17
.

The Engeler model in
V^{A}
, as described in Theorem
4.12
, is a reflexive continuous lattice with numerals, when given (
\check{{\mbox{-}}}
of) the Church Booleans and Church numerals.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.13
,
\|\llbracket{\check{\bot}}\rrbracket^{A}=\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-11.3889pt\hbox{$\llbracket{\bot}\rrbracket$}\hskip-11.3889pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{18.00012pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigvee\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\re
